Project Giga-Foundry: A Business Plan for Autonomous Solar DominanceI. Executive Summary: The Autonomous Giga-FoundryMission: To establish physical.capital as the world's first fully autonomous, vertically integrated solar manufacturer, launching from a strategic asset in Colorado Springs. This venture will achieve a 100x reduction in solar manufacturing cost-per-watt and a 10x increase in production velocity, with the ultimate goal of capturing 10-30% of the U.S. solar manufacturing market by 2030.The Opportunity: The United States is at a critical nexus. The Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) has catalyzed a massive "reshoring" of the solar supply chain, with domestic module manufacturing capacity already exceeding 60 GW.1 Simultaneously, the explosive growth of AI and cloud computing is driving unprecedented demand for energy from hyperscale datacenters. This is evidenced by market-setting deals such as Microsoft's 12 GW, multi-year off-take agreement with Qcells.3 physical.capital is positioned to seize this supply-meets-demand inflection point.The Asset: The plan hinges on the immediate acquisition of 1615 Garden of the Gods Road, a 705,000 sq ft former Intel fabrication plant. This "shovel-ready" asset includes 90+ MW of expandable power and over 120,000 sq ft of high-specification cleanroom space, eliminating 18-24 months of permitting and construction.4The "Giga-Foundry" Thesis: The recent, high-profile failure of traditional Swiss manufacturer Meyer Burger to launch its advanced HJT solar cell operations at this exact site is not a risk, but the central validation of this plan.8 It proves that conventional, high-Capex, human-led manufacturing—even with IRA support—is no longer financially viable. The only path to success is a radical "automation-first" model. This facility will be operated 24/7 by a heterogeneous workforce of humanoid robots, including Tesla Optimus, Figure, and Unitree, driving labor and opex costs to near-zero.10The "Mine-to-Module" Stack: This project will achieve unparalleled cost reduction through full vertical integration. This includes autonomous quartz mining in Colorado 13, on-site processing into polysilicon, automated cell and module fabrication at the Giga-Foundry, and autonomous trucking for logistics.14 This entire operation will be based within Colorado, which has cultivated a favorable regulatory environment for autonomous operations.15The Path to Scale: Phase 1 will be the activation of 1615 Garden of the Gods. Phase 2 will involve "copy/pasting" the perfected autonomous fab blueprint onto new-build facilities in the Peak Innovation Park at the Colorado Springs Airport.16 This site offers a "super-stack" of incentives, including Opportunity Zone status, to fund rapid expansion.18The Ask: This plan outlines the strategy to secure the Giga-Foundry 1 asset, attract the core leadership team, and secure the multi-billion-dollar, multi-gigawatt off-take agreements from hyperscale customers necessary to fund this scaled buildout.II. The Development Entity: physical.capitalProfile: physical.capital (a division of lander.media)physical.capital, a division of Lander Media 20, is the development entity founded by CEO Steve Moraco. It is structured to execute this vision. Where Lander Media leverages digital-first strategies for information-age clients 21, physical.capital will apply a similar systems-level, "first principles" approach to industrial-age physical infrastructure.physical.capital is not a traditional real estate developer or manufacturer. It is a systems integration company that designs and builds the physical infrastructure for the autonomous future. The Giga-Foundry 1 venture will be its flagship project, structured as a dedicated Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) to optimize acquisition, financing, and operations, while leveraging the intellectual capital and strategic narrative capabilities of its parent.Founder’s Vision: Steve MoracoCEO Steve Moraco's vision is to execute a "Tesla-Giga-factory" playbook on the solar industry. The core strategic analysis is that the primary bottleneck in renewable energy adoption is no longer solar panel efficiency, but manufacturing cost and speed.The central thesis is that by treating the factory itself as a product—an "autonomous manufacturing machine"—and integrating robotics and AI from the ground up, exponential cost reductions and production velocity gains are achievable. This project rejects the incrementalism of traditional manufacturing and pursues an automation-first model as the only viable path to gigascale deployment.Initial Advisory Board: The "Giga-Systems" & "Ground-Game" TeamA world-class advisory board is pre-selected to navigate the two primary pillars of this venture: gigascale physics and on-the-ground execution.Casey Handmer, PhD (Giga-Systems Advisor):Dr. Handmer is the Founder of Terraform Industries 22, a company focused on gigascale engineering to produce carbon-free synthetic fuel.24 With a background in theoretical physics from Caltech and experience at NASA JPL 23, Dr. Handmer is one of the foremost "first principles" thinkers on energy and infrastructure.26Role: Dr. Handmer's role is to advise the CEO and CTO on the physics of the 100x cost-down model. He will validate the energy-per-watt calculations, the chemical process for polysilicon refinement, the logistics-chain efficiencies, and the fundamental design of the factory as a high-velocity system.Perry Sanders (Strategic Counsel):Mr. Sanders is a Colorado Springs-based attorney with Sanders Law Firm, specializing in complex civil litigation, real estate, and regulatory frameworks, including environmental cases.27Role: Mr. Sanders will lead the critical "ground game." His immediate task is to engage the brokers for 1615 Garden of the Gods (Marcus & Millichap / Quantum Commercial 4) and negotiate the acquisition of the asset, leveraging the recent strategic failure of the previous tenant. Concurrently, he will interface with El Paso County and the City of Colorado Springs to secure the vital incentive packages for both Phase 1 (Garden of the Gods) and Phase 2 (Peak Innovation Park).18III. Strategic Asset Acquisition: Giga-Foundry 1 (1615 Garden of the Gods)Asset Analysis: The Perfect "Shell"The entire premise of a "panel in months" compressed timeline is contingent on acquiring an existing, infrastructure-rich facility. 1615 Garden of the Gods Road is not just a target; it is the only logical choice, offering a true speed-to-market advantage.4Address: 1615 Garden of the Gods Road, Colorado Springs, CO 80907.31History: Originally built by Intel as "Fab 23," this $4 billion facility was an engineering marvel.4Facility Specs: A 30.88-acre campus with a 705,000 sq ft main facility.4Power: This is the most critical and irreplaceable component. The site has 70 MW of existing power, expandable to 90+ MW, with dual feeds for redundancy.4 Securing this level of power for new construction would take years and is a primary bottleneck for all new industrial development.Cleanroom Infrastructure: The facility contains 120,000-140,000 sq ft of Class 10,000-capable cleanroom space.5 This includes 4-foot raised floors, ULPA filtration (99.99995% efficiency), and 420 air changes per hour.7 This infrastructure is perfectly suited for semiconductor-grade polysilicon processing and advanced cell manufacturing.Structural Specs: The building is an industrial fortress, featuring heavy floor load capacity (rated for 500 PSF) and exceptionally high clear heights, with sections up to 69'10".4 This is ideal for heavy robotics, large-format automated tooling, and multi-story processing equipment.Zoning: The property is zoned PIP 1 (Pikes Peak Industrial Park), explicitly allowing for heavy manufacturing and industrial use.6The Meyer Burger Precedent: A Strategic Opportunity, Not a LiabilityThe history of this site in 2023-2024 is the central justification for physical.capital's autonomous-first strategy.The Initial Plan: In August 2023, Swiss manufacturer Meyer Burger announced it would lease this facility for a 17-year, $128 million term.32 The plan was to invest over $400 million to build a 2 GW high-performance solar cell (HJT) factory, creating 350+ jobs.32 They planned a "fast track" 2024 launch by redirecting equipment from Germany 34 and were supported by a $300 million DOE loan application and $90 million in local incentives.35The Failure: By August 2024, Meyer Burger abruptly suspended the project, stating it was "currently not financially viable".8 They will now source cells from their existing German plant.9The Strategic Conclusion: Meyer Burger’s failure, despite possessing the "perfect" billion-dollar asset, massive power, a "fast track" timeline, and full federal and state-level support, proves a fundamental market truth: incremental improvements in traditional, human-centric solar manufacturing are a dead end. Their high-cost HJT technology 37, reliant on complex (but non-autonomous) tooling 39, could not compete.This failure creates an extraordinary opportunity. The property is now back on the market, fully-vetted for solar manufacturing, and its private owner is left with a long-term lease default. The City of Colorado Springs and the State of Colorado, having publicly announced 350+ jobs and a $400M investment 33, are now highly motivated to support a new, more ambitious, and—critically—more viable tenant.Acquisition & Compressed TimelineAction: Strategic Counsel Perry Sanders will immediately engage the listed brokers—Michael P. Palmer at Quantum Commercial Group 6 and The Kramer Group at Marcus & Millichap 4—to negotiate an acquisition or long-term lease.Status: The property is actively listed "For Sale or Long-Term Lease".4 An older listing flyer cited a sale price of $12 million 7, while more recent listings from 2023-2024 cite $35 million.6 The $35 million figure will serve as the basis for a negotiation, which will be heavily leveraged by the recent Meyer Burger default and the clear demonstration that only a tenant with a radically different cost structure can succeed.By acquiring this fully-permitted, power-rich shell, physical.capital eliminates 18-24 months of site selection, permitting, and utility interconnection, making a "panel in months" timeline possible.IV. The "100x Cost, 10x Speed" Thesis: A New Manufacturing ParadigmDeconstructing Solar CoGS: The 100x PathThe "100x cost-down" goal is not aspirational; it is an engineering target. Traditional solar manufacturing costs are dominated by materials (~70%), Capex, labor, and energy.41 The physical.capital model attacks all cost centers simultaneously.Labor (100x Reduction): We will design a "lights-out," 24/7/365 factory operated almost entirely by humanoid robots. This eliminates 95%+ of direct and indirect human labor costs, shift change inefficiencies, and human-introduced errors (dramatically improving process yield).Materials (5x-10x Reduction): Full vertical integration—from an autonomous mine 42 to on-site polysilicon refinement—captures the entire upstream materials margin. Furthermore, the strategic shift to Perovskite-Tandem technology (see Section V) will dramatically reduce the quantity of high-cost semiconductor material required per watt.Capex (5x Reduction): Traditional fabs are built around rigid, single-purpose, multi-million-dollar tooling for specific tasks.39 The Giga-Foundry is built around general-purpose robotics. The robots are the assembly line. This makes the entire factory programmable, flexible, and far cheaper to re-tool for new technologies or panel formats.Speed (10x Increase): A 24/7 robotic workforce has over three times the operational uptime of a three-shift human system. When combined with autonomous logistics that eliminate wait times for material handling and shipping, the "production-per-square-foot" will be an order of magnitude higher than any existing fab.The Robotic Workforce: A Multi-Vendor "AI-Cell" ApproachNo single robot can perform all factory tasks. The Giga-Foundry will deploy a heterogeneous fleet, managed by a central AI "fab-brain"—the core proprietary IP of physical.capital.Tesla Optimus (The "Worker Bee"):Status: Gen 2/3 is slated for limited production in 2025, with plans for over 1,000 units to be deployed in Tesla's own facilities.10Role: Optimus will handle high-repetition, structured tasks: loading/unloading cell carriers, stringing and bussing, moving finished-goods racks, and performing logistics. Its critical advantage is its integration with Tesla's FSD-derived AI stack, allowing it to learn and navigate a dynamic factory environment.44 physical.capital will position itself as a key "beta" customer, identical to Tesla's own factories.Figure 01 (The "Technician"):Status: Commercially deploying in 2025 through a landmark agreement with BMW Manufacturing.11Role: Figure 01 is explicitly designed to "replace human workers in physical labor roles" requiring high dexterity.11 It will perform complex, non-standard tasks: machine calibration, quality control inspections, tool swap-outs, and handling delicate materials for the advanced perovskite line.Unitree H1 / G1 (The "Patrol & Logistics"):Status: Commercially available. The H1 is a high-performance (3.3 m/s speed, 360 N.m torque) 47 platform. The G1 is a lower-cost ($16k) "AI avatar".49Role: H1 units, with their 3D LiDAR and depth cameras 12, will continuously patrol the 705,000 sq ft fab, creating a real-time digital twin, inspecting equipment for anomalies, and providing data to the fab-brain. Lower-cost G1 units will handle internal micro-logistics, such as moving individual tools or components between workstations.Achieving "Panel in Months": The Critical Path to First ProductionThe compressed timeline is achieved through aggressive parallelization, enabled by the existing asset.Month 1-2: Close on 1615 Garden of the Gods. Finalize technology path (Section V).Month 2-4: Facility retrofit. The cleanrooms are largely ready.7 Focus on installing power/chemical hookups for polysilicon processing and cell deposition equipment.Month 3-5: Receive and integrate first robotics-cells (Unitree, Figure). Begin "training" the robots on the physical space and initial tasks in parallel with the facility retrofit.Month 6-8: Tool-In & Commissioning. Install the core cell manufacturing equipment.Month 9-10: Integration & First Article. Integrate Optimus units. Run the full "mine-to-module" autonomous process. Produce first customer-ready panels.V. The Technology: Process & Material Strategy (The 100x Enabler)The 100x cost goal cannot be achieved by simply automating yesterday's technology. It requires a strategic pivot to the next generation of solar materials, enabled by automation.The Solar Technology Landscape (PERC vs. TOPCon vs. HJT)PERC: The industry standard for years. It is cheap but has hit its theoretical efficiency limit at ~23.5%.37TOPCon: The new N-type mainstream. It offers higher efficiency (24%+) and better high-temperature performance, and critically, it can be manufactured on upgraded PERC lines.52 This is the "safe," bankable choice for 2025.HJT (Heterojunction): What Meyer Burger planned to use. It offers the highest silicon efficiency (24.5%+) but involves a complex, high-cost manufacturing process.37 Its failure at our target facility 9 is a powerful market signal that its cost/complexity is too high.Path A (Bankable): N-Type TOPConThe facility could be dedicated to autonomous TOPCon production. The robotic workforce would still yield a 3-5x cost advantage, making this a highly profitable, low-risk path to securing immediate, gigawatt-scale off-take agreements.Path B (Disruptive): Perovskite-Silicon TandemThe true 100x play lies in Perovskite-Tandem cells.The Promise: Perovskites promise to "surpass the efficiency limits of silicon".56 When layered on top of a silicon cell, they create a "tandem" device that can capture more of the light spectrum, with lab efficiencies already exceeding 34%.57The Problem: Commercialization is stalled by two key challenges: 1) long-term stability and durability 58, and 2) the difficulty of scalable manufacturing (e.g., depositing the "ink" layers 60) with perfect precision.61The Autonomous Solution: These are not fundamental material problems; they are automation problems. Human-led labs and factories are too slow, imprecise, and contaminated to solve the stability issue at scale. The Giga-Foundry is the solution. The Intel-grade cleanrooms 7 provide the perfect, contaminant-free environment. The 24/7 high-dexterity robotic workforce (Figure 01, Optimus) provides the only platform capable of performing the high-precision, high-iteration deposition tasks required to mass-produce stable perovskite cells.Recommendation: The Dual-Track Strategyphysical.capital will pursue both paths simultaneously, de-risking the business while capturing the disruptive upside.Phase 1A (Months 1-12): Launch with N-Type TOPCon in 70% of the fab. This will be the "cash-cow" product. It is bankable, IRA-compliant, and exactly what our datacenter customers are already buying. This secures the initial gigawatt off-take agreements and funds operations.Phase 1B (Months 1-12): In parallel, dedicate the highest-spec cleanroom (30% of fab) to a "Perovskite Foundry" Joint Venture. physical.capital will partner with a technology leader (e.g., Oxford PV 62, Saule Technologies 60, or CubicPV 63) and act as their autonomous scaling partner. They provide the cell-stack IP; we provide the automated manufacturing platform.This dual-track strategy makes physical.capital the only company that can deliver today's bankable standard (TOPCon) at a disruptive price, while simultaneously being the only company that has "cracked the code" on mass-producing the 100x-cost-down technology of tomorrow (Perovskite).VI. Full Vertical Integration: The "Mine-to-Module" Autonomous Supply ChainUpstream: Autonomous Mining (The "Sand")Thesis: To control cost, we must control the raw material. The project will secure its own silica/quartz supply chain by acquiring and operating a Colorado-based mine.Resource: Colorado has known deposits of silica and quartz.64 High-Purity Quartz (HPQ) is now classified as a strategic commodity, essential for producing both semiconductors and solar-grade polysilicon.42The Autonomous Advantage: The mine will be operated autonomously, partnering with a leader in autonomous haulage systems such as Caterpillar, Komatsu, or Sandvik.13 This is not just a cost-saving measure; it is a profound strategic advantage.Regulatory Arbitrage: In April 2024, the Mine Safety and Health Administration (MSHA) issued a strict final rule, lowering the permissible exposure limit for respirable crystalline silica (quartz dust) to 50 µg/m³.67 This new rule, which took effect in 2025 69, imposes significant new monitoring, PPE, and engineering control costs on all traditional mining operators.The physical.capital Solution: An autonomous mine, operated by robotic haul trucks 70 and excavators, has no human workers in the "pit." Therefore, this project completely bypasses the primary cost and health/safety liability of silica mining. A regulatory headwind that crushes competitors' margins becomes a massive tailwind for physical.capital.Midstream: The Autonomous "Sand-to-Panel" Corridor (The "Logistics")Thesis: The project will connect the mine to Giga-Foundry 1 (Garden of the Gods) and Giga-Foundry 2 (Peak Innovation Park) using a fully autonomous trucking fleet.Technology: A partnership will be established with a SAE Level 4 autonomous trucking leader like Aurora Innovation.14 Aurora is already running fully driverless commercial routes (e.g., Dallas-Houston, Fort Worth-El Paso) and plans to deploy hundreds of trucks by 2026.14The "Colorado Green Light": This vision is uniquely viable in Colorado. In May 2025, Governor Jared Polis vetoed HB25-1122, a bill that would have banned autonomous trucks from operating without a human CDL holder in the cab.15 In his veto letter, Polis explicitly stated the bill would "undermine innovation."This veto is not a minor detail; it is an explicit invitation from the state's executive. It creates a "green-light" regulatory environment. physical.capital will leverage this to build the nation's first fully autonomous, heavy-material, mine-to-manufacturing-plant supply route, likely along the I-25 corridor.Downstream: Autonomous ShipmentThe same autonomous fleet will be used for downstream logistics, shipping finished modules 24/7 from the Giga-Foundry directly to customer datacenter construction sites.VII. Project Götterdämmerung: A Business Plan for the Autonomous, Vertically-Integrated Production of Solar EnergyThis document presents the strategic business plan for Physical Capital to execute on its vision of autonomous solar panel development. The plan, codenamed "Project Götterdämmerung," leverages a unique, time-sensitive distressed asset opportunity to establish a "lights-out" manufacturing facility, vertically integrate a domestic supply chain, and capture a dominant share of the U.S. solar market, with an initial focus on hyperscale datacenter offtakers.I. The Founding Mandate: Physical Capital and the Disruption of Energy ManufacturingThis section establishes the corporate, strategic, and narrative foundation of the venture, leveraging the unique history and capabilities of Lander.media and Physical Capital.Corporate Narrative & HistoryThe venture will be led by Physical Capital (founded 2016), a development company operating as a division of Lander.media (founded 2014) [User Query]. This structure provides a distinct strategic advantage: Physical Capital is structured to manage the complex, capital-intensive infrastructure syndication and development, while Lander.media, as the parent entity, possesses the "story engineering" 1 capability required to market this revolutionary manufacturing approach.The venture's brand identity will be built on the personal ethos of its CEO, Steve Moraco, defined by an "impatient and ambitious" 1 approach focused on "always coding, systematizing, and automating".1 This positioning frames the project not as a legacy industrial endeavor, but as a high-speed, founder-led disruption of the energy manufacturing sector.A History of Catalysis: The Swisspod/TTCI PlaybookPhysical Capital's documented success from 2016 to 2024 in bringing Swisspod, a European hyperloop company, to Colorado Springs serves as the foundational playbook for this project.2This prior success demonstrates a core competency in navigating complex, multi-year, multi-stakeholder infrastructure projects. The Swisspod relocation was not a simple real estate transaction; it required high-level coordination between:State Government: Governor Jared Polis and the Colorado Office of Economic Development and International Trade (OEDIT).2Local Government: The City of Colorado Springs and Mayor Yemi Mobalade.4Federal/Industry Partners: MxV Rail (formerly the federal Transportation Technology Center, Inc. or TTCI) in Pueblo.5Project Götterdämmerung will require an identical syndication effort: coordinating with OEDIT for state incentives 7, the City of Colorado Springs for local support and expedited permitting 9, and navigating federal programs, specifically the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) 45X credits and potential Department of Energy (DOE) Loan Programs Office (LPO) financing. This history establishes Physical Capital as a proven catalyst for complex technology infrastructure in the state.Mission: The 100x Cost & 10x Speed MandateThe mission is to achieve a 100x reduction in the net cost of solar manufacturing and a 10x increase in the speed of factory deployment [User Query].These objectives are not based on marginal operational improvements but on a radical financial and operational re-architecture.The 100x Cost Reduction: This is a financial engineering objective. It will be achieved by aggressively "stacking" the IRA's Section 45X Advanced Manufacturing Production Credits 10 to create a "negative cost" product (detailed in Section V). This is combined with the operational goal of eliminating direct labor costs via "lights-out" autonomous manufacturing.12The 10x Speed Increase: This objective targets deployment, not production. It will be achieved by:Brownfield Acceleration: Acquiring the distressed manufacturing assets of Meyer Burger, thereby bypassing 12-18 months of equipment procurement lead times.14Robotic Replication: Using the initial autonomous factory as an R&D hub to design, build, and deploy new, fully automated factories ("factories building factories") at scale at the Peak Innovation Park.9The "story engineering" capability of Lander.media 1 is the asymmetric weapon that distinguishes this venture. Competitors (e.g., First Solar, Chinese manufacturers) sell solar panels as a commodity based on price-per-watt. Physical Capital will sell a narrative that is highly valuable to its target customers (Google, Meta, Microsoft). This narrative—an American industrial rebirth, a "Phoenix" factory resurrected from a competitor's failure, run by humanoid robots—transforms the product from a simple panel into a strategic marketing asset for the customer's own brand and sustainability goals.17II. The Keystone Asset: Acquiring the 1615 Garden of the Gods "Phoenix" FacilityThis section details the primary, time-sensitive acquisition strategy. The "panels in months" timeline is only achievable by executing this specific distressed asset play.Strategic Analysis of the "Alpha" FacilityAsset: 1615 Garden of the Gods Road, Colorado Springs, CO.19Specifications: A 705,000-square-foot campus on a 30.88-acre lot, originally built by Intel as a semiconductor fab for over $4 billion.19Key Infrastructure: This asset is irreplaceable for the project's purposes. It provides infrastructure that would take years and hundreds of millions of dollars to replicate:Power: 70-90+ MW of existing power capacity with dual feeds, essential for 24/7 "lights-out" manufacturing.19Cleanroom: 120,000 square feet of Class 10,000-capable cleanroom space, perfectly suited for solar cell production.19Zoning: Zoned PIP 1 (Planned Industrial Park), which explicitly allows for "Light Manufacturing" and "Data Center" uses.21The Meyer Burger Default: A Critical Path AcceleratorThe immediate opportunity stems from the spectacular collapse of the previous tenant, Meyer Burger.The Original Lease: In August 2023, Swiss manufacturer Meyer Burger signed a 17-year, $128 million lease for 773,713 square feet at the facility.24 This was a cornerstone of the local industrial market.25The Failed Plan: Meyer Burger intended to invest $400 million to convert the fab into a 2 GW high-performance solar cell factory.24 This facility was designated to be the exclusive cell supplier for their "Made in USA" solar module assembly plant in Goodyear, AZ.24The Collapse (2024-2025): The venture failed rapidly due to market pressures (detailed below), culminating in a complete U.S. withdrawal.August 2024: Meyer Burger officially canceled the $400 million Colorado Springs project, deeming it "no longer financially viable".28May 2025: The company shut down its primary Arizona module factory and laid off 282 employees.30September 2025: The company, in bankruptcy, was forced to sell off its U.S. manufacturing assets.14Operation "Babacomari": The Distressed Asset Acquisition LynchpinThis is the central finding of this plan and the key to its accelerated timeline.The Asset Sale: In September 2025, Meyer Burger's assets were auctioned. The module production equipment in Arizona was sold to Waaree Solar Americas.14The Key Target: The solar cell production equipment—the 2 GW Heterojunction (HJT) line purchased for the Colorado Springs plant—was sold to Babacomari Solar North LLC.14The Buyer: Babacomari Solar North LLC is a Delaware-based entity 34 who was the "Secured Creditor" in the foreclosure.36 They acquired the assets not with cash, but via a $10.2 million credit bid.37The Counsel: The legal representative for Babacomari in this UCC foreclosure sale was Robert E. Richards of the law firm Dentons US LLP.36The "panels in months" goal is impossible without acquiring this specific, unused 2 GW cell line from Babacomari. A new turnkey production line has an 8-10 month lead time at minimum.15 Meyer Burger already completed this 12-18 month procurement cycle. By acquiring these crated assets, Physical Capital is buying back 18 months of time.This situation creates two powerful points of negotiation leverage.Asset Leverage: Babacomari is a solar developer 38, not a cell manufacturer. They are holding highly specialized HJT cell equipment 14 that is useless to them, as Waaree bought the module factory it was meant to supply.14 This equipment is a non-cash, depreciating asset on their books from a credit bid. They are highly motivated to liquidate it for cash.Real Estate Leverage: The private owner of 1615 GotG just lost its $128 million, 17-year anchor tenant.24 The property is now a vacant, highly-specialized, and expensive-to-maintain fab.19 The brokers (Marcus & Millichap 20, Binswanger 24) have an urgent problem. Physical Capital is the only logical party that can simultaneously solve the landlord's tenant problem and Babacomari's asset problem.A final strategic point is the reason for Meyer Burger's failure. Their assets are for Heterojunction (HJT) technology 31, a high-efficiency N-type technology.41 However, it is a more complex and expensive manufacturing process than the dominant Chinese N-type technology, TOPCon.42 As of late 2025, Chinese TOPCon modules are selling for as low as $0.088/W 45, while US-made panels are over $0.28/W.46 Meyer Burger could not compete. This would be a fatal flaw, except for the financial model in Section V. The 45X credits are so large that they erase this manufacturing cost differential, allowing Physical Capital to acquire high-efficiency HJT assets at a fire-sale price while being financially immune to the very market forces that bankrupted their predecessor.Action Plan (First 30 Days)Engage Perry Sanders (Legal): Immediately, to structure the acquisition.Contact Robert E. Richards (Dentons): Sanders' first call. Open negotiations to acquire the Babacomari-held solar cell assets.36Contact Max Rist (Marcus & Millichap): Second call. Open negotiations to assume the distressed 1615 GotG lease at highly favorable terms.20Issue LOI: Submit simultaneous Letters of Intent for both the assets and the real estate, making each acquisition contingent upon the other.III. The "Lights-Out" Factory: A Phased Approach to Autonomous ManufacturingThis section details the pragmatic, "hybrid" automation strategy, reconciling the "panels in months" timeline with the R&D-phase status of general-purpose humanoid robots.Phase 1: The "Humanoid-Assisted" Line (Months 0-9)The vision of a factory run entirely by Tesla Optimus and Figure robots is not viable in the 2025-2026 timeframe.Robotics Status:Tesla Optimus: Remains in R&D. While 2025 production targets were 5,000-10,000 units 47, actual production is estimated at "a few hundred".48 The robots are not "employment standards" ready and face "core technology bottlenecks".48 They are not available for mass procurement.Figure AI: Currently in early commercial pilots (e.g., with BMW) 50 but is not yet a commercially-hardened, mass-market product.51The Hybrid Solution: The "lights-out" factory 13 will be achieved by combining mature, specialized robotics for the core process with advanced, general-purpose robotics for high-value ancillary tasks.Core Process (Module Assembly): The Babacomari assets are for cell production. A module assembly line must be added. A 2 GW turnkey automated module line will be sourced from established vendors such as:Ecoprogetti: Proven in the U.S. (delivered an 800 MW line in Texas 54), fully UL-compliant, and offers high-speed (200 modules/hour) automated lines.55LEAD Intelligent: Offers GW-level smart factories compatible with HJT technology.57Industrial Arms: The line will be integrated with "lights-out" production cells from established leaders like ABB 12 and KUKA 13, both of which have proven solutions in the solar industry.12This line will automate the key process steps: cell tabbing 61, stringing 62, layup 64, bussing 65, lamination, and framing.Advanced Robotics Deployment (The "Humanoid" Role): An "Autonomous Robotics" division will be established to partner directly with Figure and Tesla. The Physical Capital factory will serve as their first large-scale industrial customer, granting them invaluable operational data in exchange for priority access and engineering support. Their roles will be:Figure 02: High-dexterity tasks. Primary role: Wafer Cassette Handling. The Figure robot will autonomously move delicate wafer cassettes from the HJT cell line (Babacomari assets) to the module line (Ecoprogetti assets). This is a known automation bottleneck that typically requires human-like dexterity.66Tesla Optimus: Logistics and "heavy lift" tasks.70 Primary roles: 1) Line Replenishment: Autonomously hauling consumables (EVA, backsheets, glass) to the line. 2) Final Crating: Lifting finished 50-lb+ panels and palletizing/crating them for shipment. This aligns perfectly with Tesla's own intended factory use cases.47Unitree B1: Autonomous Facility Inspection. A fleet of B1 quadrupeds 73 will patrol the 700,000 sq. ft. facility 24/7. Their IP68 rating 74 and advanced sensor suites (thermal, 3D lidar) 76 will be used for autonomous inspection of power substations, HVAC systems, and cleanroom perimeters, replacing human security and maintenance patrols.76This hybrid approach secures the "panels in months" timeline by using mature robotics (Ecoprogetti, ABB) for the core line, while simultaneously securing the "story engineering" 1 and long-term IP by partnering with Tesla and Figure to automate the ancillary tasks.Phase 2: The Self-Replicating Factory (The "10x Speed" Goal)The "10x speed" goal is not about production line tact time; it is about factory replication time. The 12-18 month timeline to build a new greenfield factory is the real industry bottleneck.15The 1615 GotG "Alpha" factory will serve as the R&D hub for manufacturing automation itself. The humanoid robots and automation teams will be tasked with designing, building, and deploying the next set of production lines. The 10x speed goal is to use our robots to build more robots, shrinking the "time-to-new-gigafactory" from 18 months to less than 6.The 10-30% Market Share Goal (Market Sizing)US Capacity (2025): Total domestic module manufacturing capacity is ~55.4 GW.78US Installations (2024/2025): The U.S. is installing ~40-50 GW of solar capacity per year.7910-30% Target: This equates to a production target of 5.5 GW to 16.6 GW of annual production capacity.This target will be met via a phased scaling plan, which is financially in line with market precedents like First Solar's $1.2B plan for 10 GW 82 and NorSun's $620M plan for 5-10 GW.83Year 1 (Alpha Site): 2 GW (1615 Garden of the Gods facility).Year 2-3 (Beta Site): 5 GW (Peak Innovation Park, Fab 1).Year 4-5 (Gamma Site): 10 GW (Peak Innovation Park, Fab 2).IV. The Financial Core: The IRA 45X Giga-Credit ModelThis section details the financial engine of the venture. The "100x cost reduction" goal is not a manufacturing target; it is a financial engineering target. This model makes the venture immune to the market forces that bankrupted Meyer Burger.31Deconstructing the "100x Cost" ClaimThe strategy is to use "lights-out" automation (Section III) to minimize direct labor costs and use the IRA 45X Advanced Manufacturing Production Credit 10 to achieve a negative cash cost of goods sold (COGS).The 45X "Giga-Stack": A Production-Based Revenue StreamThe IRA 45X credit is a production-based credit, not an investment-based one.11 The manufacturer is compensated for every eligible component it produces and sells in the U.S..136 The phased vertical integration plan (Section IV) is designed specifically to "stack" these credits at every stage of the supply chain.11The financial model below demonstrates how the stacked credits can exceed the total sale price of competing modules.Table: IRA 45X Giga-Stack Financial Model (Per 500W Panel)Production StageComponentIRA 45X Credit RateUnits per 500W Module (Est.)Stacked Credit ($)Phase 2Polysilicon$3.00 / kg 101.5 kg$4.50Phase 1Wafer$0.12 / sq. meter 101.5 sq. meters$0.18Phase 1Cell$0.04 / watt 10500 watts$20.00Phase 1Module$0.07 / watt 10500 watts$35.00TOTAL$59.68Market Analysis (Competitor COGS):Chinese TOPCon Module (FOB China Price, Q4 2025): $0.088/W.45Competitor 500W Panel Price: $44.00Conclusion:The tax credit revenue per panel ($59.68) is 135% of the competitor's total sale price ($44.00).This financial structure allows Physical Capital to sell its superior, US-made panels at any price—even $0.01/W—and remain profitable. This is the 100x cost reduction. This model is not just competitive with, but fundamentally breaks, the global commodity market.Cash Flow via Transferability (The "First Solar" Model)This model does not require $60 of tax liability per panel. The IRA allows for "transferability," enabling the sale of these credits for cash.138Precedent: First Solar sold $700 million of its 2023 45X credits to Fiserv for $0.96 on the dollar.141Strategy: The 45X credits will be treated as a primary cash revenue stream.Financial Impact: A 2 GW factory (the Alpha site) producing at capacity will generate $280,000,000 per year in module credits alone ($0.07/W * 2,000,000,000W). Stacking all credits (Phase 3) will more than double this. This non-dilutive financing will fund all CapEx for the expansion at Peak Innovation Park.VI. Project Götterdämmerung: A Business Plan for the Autonomous, Vertically-Integrated Production of Solar EnergyThis document presents the strategic business plan for Physical Capital to execute on its vision of autonomous solar panel development. The plan, codenamed "Project Götterdämmerung," leverages a unique, time-sensitive distressed asset opportunity to establish a "lights-out" manufacturing facility, vertically integrate a domestic supply chain, and capture a dominant share of the U.S. solar market, with an initial focus on hyperscale datacenter offtakers.I. The Founding Mandate: Physical Capital and the Disruption of Energy ManufacturingThis section establishes the corporate, strategic, and narrative foundation of the venture, leveraging the unique history and capabilities of Lander.media and Physical Capital.Corporate Narrative & HistoryThe venture will be led by Physical Capital (founded 2016), a development company operating as a division of Lander.media (founded 2014) [User Query]. This structure provides a distinct strategic advantage: Physical Capital is structured to manage the complex, capital-intensive infrastructure syndication and development, while Lander.media, as the parent entity, possesses the "story engineering" 1 capability required to market this revolutionary manufacturing approach.The venture's brand identity will be built on the personal ethos of its CEO, Steve Moraco, defined by an "impatient and ambitious" 1 approach focused on "always coding, systematizing, and automating".1 This positioning frames the project not as a legacy industrial endeavor, but as a high-speed, founder-led disruption of the energy manufacturing sector.A History of Catalysis: The Swisspod/TTCI PlaybookPhysical Capital's documented success from 2016 to 2024 in bringing Swisspod, a European hyperloop company, to Colorado Springs serves as the foundational playbook for this project.2This prior success demonstrates a core competency in navigating complex, multi-year, multi-stakeholder infrastructure projects. The Swisspod relocation was not a simple real estate transaction; it required high-level coordination between:State Government: Governor Jared Polis and the Colorado Office of Economic Development and International Trade (OEDIT).2Local Government: The City of Colorado Springs and Mayor Yemi Mobalade.4Federal/Industry Partners: MxV Rail (formerly the federal Transportation Technology Center, Inc. or TTCI) in Pueblo.5Project Götterdämmerung will require an identical syndication effort: coordinating with OEDIT for state incentives 7, the City of Colorado Springs for local support and expedited permitting 9, and navigating federal programs, specifically the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) 45X credits and potential Department of Energy (DOE) Loan Programs Office (LPO) financing. This history establishes Physical Capital as a proven catalyst for complex technology infrastructure in the state.Mission: The 100x Cost & 10x Speed MandateThe mission is to achieve a 100x reduction in the net cost of solar manufacturing and a 10x increase in the speed of factory deployment [User Query].These objectives are not based on marginal operational improvements but on a radical financial and operational re-architecture.The 100x Cost Reduction: This is a financial engineering objective. It will be achieved by aggressively "stacking" the IRA's Section 45X Advanced Manufacturing Production Credits 10 to create a "negative cost" product (detailed in Section V). This is combined with the operational goal of eliminating direct labor costs via "lights-out" autonomous manufacturing.12The 10x Speed Increase: This objective targets deployment, not production. It will be achieved by:Brownfield Acceleration: Acquiring the distressed manufacturing assets of Meyer Burger, thereby bypassing 12-18 months of equipment procurement lead times.14Robotic Replication: Using the initial autonomous factory as an R&D hub to design, build, and deploy new, fully automated factories ("factories building factories") at scale at the Peak Innovation Park.9The "story engineering" capability of Lander.media 1 is the asymmetric weapon that distinguishes this venture. Competitors (e.g., First Solar, Chinese manufacturers) sell solar panels as a commodity based on price-per-watt. Physical Capital will sell a narrative that is highly valuable to its target customers (Google, Meta, Microsoft). This narrative—an American industrial rebirth, a "Phoenix" factory resurrected from a competitor's failure, run by humanoid robots—transforms the product from a simple panel into a strategic marketing asset for the customer's own brand and sustainability goals.17II. The Keystone Asset: Acquiring the 1615 Garden of the Gods "Phoenix" FacilityThis section details the primary, time-sensitive acquisition strategy. The "panels in months" timeline is only achievable by executing this specific distressed asset play.Strategic Analysis of the "Alpha" FacilityAsset: 1615 Garden of the Gods Road, Colorado Springs, CO.19Specifications: A 705,000-square-foot campus on a 30.88-acre lot, originally built by Intel as a semiconductor fab for over $4 billion.19Key Infrastructure: This asset is irreplaceable for the project's purposes. It provides infrastructure that would take years and hundreds of millions of dollars to replicate:Power: 70-90+ MW of existing power capacity with dual feeds, essential for 24/7 "lights-out" manufacturing.19Cleanroom: 120,000 square feet of Class 10,000-capable cleanroom space, perfectly suited for solar cell production.19Zoning: Zoned PIP 1 (Planned Industrial Park), which explicitly allows for "Light Manufacturing" and "Data Center" uses.21The Meyer Burger Default: A Critical Path AcceleratorThe immediate opportunity stems from the spectacular collapse of the previous tenant, Meyer Burger.The Original Lease: In August 2023, Swiss manufacturer Meyer Burger signed a 17-year, $128 million lease for 773,713 square feet at the facility.24 This was a cornerstone of the local industrial market.25The Failed Plan: Meyer Burger intended to invest $400 million to convert the fab into a 2 GW high-performance solar cell factory.24 This facility was designated to be the exclusive cell supplier for their "Made in USA" solar module assembly plant in Goodyear, AZ.24The Collapse (2024-2025): The venture failed rapidly due to market pressures (detailed below), culminating in a complete U.S. withdrawal.August 2024: Meyer Burger officially canceled the $400 million Colorado Springs project, deeming it "no longer financially viable".28May 2025: The company shut down its primary Arizona module factory and laid off 282 employees.30September 2025: The company, in bankruptcy, was forced to sell off its U.S. manufacturing assets.14Operation "Babacomari": The Distressed Asset Acquisition LynchpinThis is the central finding of this plan and the key to its accelerated timeline.The Asset Sale: In September 2025, Meyer Burger's assets were auctioned. The module production equipment in Arizona was sold to Waaree Solar Americas.14The Key Target: The solar cell production equipment—the 2 GW Heterojunction (HJT) line purchased for the Colorado Springs plant—was sold to Babacomari Solar North LLC.14The Buyer: Babacomari Solar North LLC is a Delaware-based entity 34 who was the "Secured Creditor" in the foreclosure.36 They acquired the assets not with cash, but via a $10.2 million credit bid.37The Counsel: The legal representative for Babacomari in this UCC foreclosure sale was Robert E. Richards of the law firm Dentons US LLP.36The "panels in months" goal is impossible without acquiring this specific, unused 2 GW cell line from Babacomari. A new turnkey production line has an 8-10 month lead time at minimum.15 Meyer Burger already completed this 12-18 month procurement cycle. By acquiring these crated assets, Physical Capital is buying back 18 months of time.This situation creates two powerful points of negotiation leverage.Asset Leverage: Babacomari is a solar developer 38, not a cell manufacturer. They are holding highly specialized HJT cell equipment 14 that is useless to them, as Waaree bought the module factory it was meant to supply.14 This equipment is a non-cash, depreciating asset on their books from a credit bid. They are highly motivated to liquidate it for cash.Real Estate Leverage: The private owner of 1615 GotG just lost its $128 million, 17-year anchor tenant.24 The property is now a vacant, highly-specialized, and expensive-to-maintain fab.19 The brokers (Marcus & Millichap 20, Binswanger 24) have an urgent problem. Physical Capital is the only logical party that can simultaneously solve the landlord's tenant problem and Babacomari's asset problem.A final strategic point is the reason for Meyer Burger's failure. Their assets are for Heterojunction (HJT) technology 31, a high-efficiency N-type technology.41 However, it is a more complex and expensive manufacturing process than the dominant Chinese N-type technology, TOPCon.42 As of late 2025, Chinese TOPCon modules are selling for as low as $0.088/W 45, while US-made panels are over $0.28/W.46 Meyer Burger could not compete. This would be a fatal flaw, except for the financial model in Section V. The 45X credits are so large that they erase this manufacturing cost differential, allowing Physical Capital to acquire high-efficiency HJT assets at a fire-sale price while being financially immune to the very market forces that bankrupted their predecessor.Action Plan (First 30 Days)Engage Perry Sanders (Legal): Immediately, to structure the acquisition.Contact Robert E. Richards (Dentons): Sanders' first call. Open negotiations to acquire the Babacomari-held solar cell assets.36Contact Max Rist (Marcus & Millichap): Second call. Open negotiations to assume the distressed 1615 GotG lease at highly favorable terms.20Issue LOI: Submit simultaneous Letters of Intent for both the assets and the real estate, making each acquisition contingent upon the other.III. The "Lights-Out" Factory: A Phased Approach to Autonomous ManufacturingThis section details the pragmatic, "hybrid" automation strategy, reconciling the "panels in months" timeline with the R&D-phase status of general-purpose humanoid robots.Phase 1: The "Humanoid-Assisted" Line (Months 0-9)The vision of a factory run entirely by Tesla Optimus and Figure robots is not viable in the 2025-2026 timeframe.Robotics Status:Tesla Optimus: Remains in R&D. While 2025 production targets were 5,000-10,000 units 47, actual production is estimated at "a few hundred".48 The robots are not "employment standards" ready and face "core technology bottlenecks".48 They are not available for mass procurement.Figure AI: Currently in early commercial pilots (e.g., with BMW) 50 but is not yet a commercially-hardened, mass-market product.51The Hybrid Solution: The "lights-out" factory 13 will be achieved by combining mature, specialized robotics for the core process with advanced, general-purpose robotics for high-value ancillary tasks.Core Process (Module Assembly): The Babacomari assets are for cell production. A module assembly line must be added. A 2 GW turnkey automated module line will be sourced from established vendors such as:Ecoprogetti: Proven in the U.S. (delivered an 800 MW line in Texas 54), fully UL-compliant, and offers high-speed (200 modules/hour) automated lines.55LEAD Intelligent: Offers GW-level smart factories compatible with HJT technology.57Industrial Arms: The line will be integrated with "lights-out" production cells from established leaders like ABB 12 and KUKA 13, both of which have proven solutions in the solar industry.12This line will automate the key process steps: cell tabbing 61, stringing 62, layup 64, bussing 65, lamination, and framing.Advanced Robotics Deployment (The "Humanoid" Role): An "Autonomous Robotics" division will be established to partner directly with Figure and Tesla. The Physical Capital factory will serve as their first large-scale industrial customer, granting them invaluable operational data in exchange for priority access and engineering support. Their roles will be:Figure 02: High-dexterity tasks. Primary role: Wafer Cassette Handling. The Figure robot will autonomously move delicate wafer cassettes from the HJT cell line (Babacomari assets) to the module line (Ecoprogetti assets). This is a known automation bottleneck that typically requires human-like dexterity.66Tesla Optimus: Logistics and "heavy lift" tasks.70 Primary roles: 1) Line Replenishment: Autonomously hauling consumables (EVA, backsheets, glass) to the line. 2) Final Crating: Lifting finished 50-lb+ panels and palletizing/crating them for shipment. This aligns perfectly with Tesla's own intended factory use cases.47Unitree B1: Autonomous Facility Inspection. A fleet of B1 quadrupeds 73 will patrol the 700,000 sq. ft. facility 24/7. Their IP68 rating 74 and advanced sensor suites (thermal, 3D lidar) 76 will be used for autonomous inspection of power substations, HVAC systems, and cleanroom perimeters, replacing human security and maintenance patrols.76This hybrid approach secures the "panels in months" timeline by using mature robotics (Ecoprogetti, ABB) for the core line, while simultaneously securing the "story engineering" 1 and long-term IP by partnering with Tesla and Figure to automate the ancillary tasks.Phase 2: The Self-Replicating Factory (The "10x Speed" Goal)The "10x speed" goal is not about production line tact time; it is about factory replication time. The 12-18 month timeline to build a new greenfield factory is the real industry bottleneck.15The 1615 GotG "Alpha" factory will serve as the R&D hub for manufacturing automation itself. The humanoid robots and automation teams will be tasked with designing, building, and deploying the next set of production lines. The 10x speed goal is to use our robots to build more robots, shrinking the "time-to-new-gigafactory" from 18 months to less than 6.The 10-30% Market Share Goal (Market Sizing)US Capacity (2025): Total domestic module manufacturing capacity is ~55.4 GW.78US Installations (2024/2025): The U.S. is installing ~40-50 GW of solar capacity per year.7910-30% Target: This equates to a production target of 5.5 GW to 16.6 GW of annual production capacity.This target will be met via a phased scaling plan, which is financially in line with market precedents like First Solar's $1.2B plan for 10 GW 82 and NorSun's $620M plan for 5-10 GW.83Year 1 (Alpha Site): 2 GW (1615 Garden of the Gods facility).Year 2-3 (Beta Site): 5 GW (Peak Innovation Park, Fab 1).Year 4-5 (Gamma Site): 10 GW (Peak Innovation Park, Fab 2).IV. The Financial Core: The IRA 45X Giga-Credit ModelThis section details the financial engine of the venture. The "100x cost reduction" goal is not a manufacturing target; it is a financial engineering target. This model makes the venture immune to the market forces that bankrupted Meyer Burger.31Deconstructing the "100x Cost" ClaimThe strategy is to use "lights-out" automation (Section III) to minimize direct labor costs and use the IRA 45X Advanced Manufacturing Production Credit 10 to achieve a negative cash cost of goods sold (COGS).The 45X "Giga-Stack": A Production-Based Revenue StreamThe IRA 45X credit is a production-based credit, not an investment-based one.11 The manufacturer is compensated for every eligible component it produces and sells in the U.S..136 The phased vertical integration plan (Section IV) is designed specifically to "stack" these credits at every stage of the supply chain.11The financial model below demonstrates how the stacked credits can exceed the total sale price of competing modules.Table: IRA 45X Giga-Stack Financial Model (Per 500W Panel)Production StageComponentIRA 45X Credit RateUnits per 500W Module (Est.)Stacked Credit ($)Phase 2Polysilicon$3.00 / kg 101.5 kg$4.50Phase 1Wafer$0.12 / sq. meter 101.5 sq. meters$0.18Phase 1Cell$0.04 / watt 10500 watts$20.00Phase 1Module$0.07 / watt 10500 watts$35.00TOTAL$59.68Market Analysis (Competitor COGS):Chinese TOPCon Module (FOB China Price, Q4 2025): $0.088/W.45Competitor 500W Panel Price: $44.00Conclusion:The tax credit revenue per panel ($59.68) is 135% of the competitor's total sale price ($44.00).This financial structure allows Physical Capital to sell its superior, US-made panels at any price—even $0.01/W—and remain profitable. This is the 100x cost reduction. This model is not just competitive with, but fundamentally breaks, the global commodity market.Cash Flow via Transferability (The "First Solar" Model)This model does not require $60 of tax liability per panel. The IRA allows for "transferability," enabling the sale of these credits for cash.138Precedent: First Solar sold $700 million of its 2023 45X credits to Fiserv for $0.96 on the dollar.141Strategy: The 45X credits will be treated as a primary cash revenue stream.Financial Impact: A 2 GW factory (the Alpha site) producing at capacity will generate $280,000,000 per year in module credits alone ($0.07/W * 2,000,000,000W). Stacking all credits (Phase 3) will more than double this. This non-dilutive financing will fund all CapEx for the expansion at Peak Innovation Park.VI. DatacenterPlacement
# A hard‑edged thesis for InterQuest–Colorado Springs as a resilient AI/datacenter cluster, fed by Eastern Colorado solar and anchored by local module manufacturing

The core argument is simple: the October 20, 2025 US‑EAST‑1 failure exposed the systemic fragility of hyperscaler concentration in Northern Virginia; a single regional control‑plane fault propagated across 100+ services for most of a day. The incident root‑caused to AWS automation corrupting DNS for DynamoDB in **us‑east‑1**, with wide cross‑service blast radius, and it was at least the third major internet event in five years linked to that cluster. Any enterprise that continues to treat US‑EAST‑1 as its de facto default is choosing correlated risk. InterQuest (north Colorado Springs) can be the counter‑example: a deliberately **regionally independent** compute hub with power-price advantages during solar‑heavy hours, proximate utility‑scale PV on the Eastern Plains, and a local, domestic-content module supply chain at **1615 Garden of the Gods**. The combination is defensible on reliability, cost, timeline, and policy incentives—and it does not depend on wishful robotics timelines. ([Amazon Web Services, Inc.][1])

## 1) The failure mode we are solving

The October 20 event wasn’t about lack of megawatts or a storm; it was software automation corrupting DNS for a foundational service (DynamoDB) in **us‑east‑1**, which in turn crippled dozens of dependent services for ~14 hours. AWS’s post‑event summary makes the causality explicit; independent analyses show impact across EC2‑adjacent subsystems and widespread customer downtime. Concentration in a single mega‑region amplified an otherwise bounded defect into an internet‑scale outage. The lesson is not “multi‑AZ”; it is “**multi‑region with independent failure domains**,” ideally in **different utilities, climates, and policy regimes**. ([Amazon Web Services, Inc.][1])

## 2) Why InterQuest in Colorado Springs is the right locus—not perfect, but better‑shaped risk

InterQuest sits on the north side of the city with direct I‑25 access, in a corridor that already hosts enterprise data centers (SAP, Walmart, FedEx, Progressive) and national colo capacity (QTS nearby at Rockrimmon; NOVVA in‑market). This matters: it proves fiber reach and makes pre‑construction due diligence faster. Zayo’s Front Range network is dense; Denver is a major interconnect market; diverse long‑haul exists along I‑25/I‑70. None of this makes InterQuest “special,” but it means you are not inventing a market from scratch. ([Baxtel][2])

On power, **Colorado Springs Utilities (CSU)** is a vertically integrated municipal utility investing heavily in new capacity and grid nodes—most visibly the **Kelker Substation expansion** (largest in the city when complete) and broader infrastructure upgrades. CSU has moved to **time‑of‑day tariffs** and even publishes an “Energy Wise Plus” profile with a **midday saver window (9 a.m.–1 p.m.)**—exactly when Eastern Plains PV floods the system. For a compute operator that can move flexible work (training, batch analytics) into those windows, the rate structure is a latent advantage, not a constraint. The caveat: CSU is tightening requirements for **>10 MW** “large‑load” interconnections (ten‑year commitments, collateral, and cost responsibility), which you need to underwrite early instead of discovering at procurement. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][3])

Climate helps the PUE story. Springs’ annual normals average ~50 °F with large diurnal swings; that is free‑cooling territory for much of the year, and ASHRAE‑aligned inlet temperatures plus air‑side economization can slash compressor hours. In practice, well‑engineered designs have achieved near‑continuous economization with PUE near **~1.07** in comparable U.S. climates; the takeaway is not a promise of “zero chillers,” but that the region’s dry, cool nights are **engineering leverage**. (Do not ignore hail—mitigate with uprated roof assemblies and louver/coil protection—and treat WUI wildfire risk as a siting/air‑intake design problem, not a reason to avoid the market.) ([Weather.gov][4])

## 3) Where the electrons come from (and why Eastern Colorado is the right match)

Colorado’s **Eastern Plains** are already being wired for large‑scale renewables through **Xcel’s “Colorado’s Power Pathway,”** a multi‑segment 345 kV build explicitly intended to pull wind/solar into the Front Range load centers. On the ground, major PV is already operating or advancing: **Bighorn Solar (300 MW, Pueblo)** and **Thunder Wolf (≈200 MW PV + 100 MW storage)**, with additional projects like **Ebba Solar (up to 300 MW + storage) near Limon** in queue. The system’s physics are clear: large PV output is midday‑skewed; CSU’s tariff now makes midday structurally cheaper; your **datacenter can monetize that spread**—if and only if your workload and contracts actually move with it. ([Xcel Energy Colorado Power Pathway][5])

Two uncomfortable but manageable realities:

1. **Jurisdictional seams.** InterQuest sits in **CSU** territory; much of the Plains PV interconnects to **PSCo/Xcel**. You will need either CSU‑sourced renewable supply, a **contract wheeling** arrangement, or a contract‑for‑differences structure that maps PV output to your load without violating local tariff rules. CSU’s materials explicitly contemplate wheeling service; build this into commercial design, not as an afterthought. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][6])
2. **Water tradeoffs for AI cooling.** High‑density AI halls push operators toward evaporative or hybrid systems that improve PUE while worsening WUE. In a semi‑arid climate, you should assume **air‑cooled or hybrid designs with strict WUE limits**, and make up the PUE delta by shifting work into low‑carbon, low‑price hours. The policy and reputational risk of high WUE in Colorado is non‑trivial. ([Environmental and Energy Study Institute][7])

## 4) Why putting module manufacturing at **1615 Garden of the Gods** in the same metro is not window‑dressing

The former Intel campus is one of the very few U.S. facilities with **70 MW installed (expandable to 90+ MW)**, cleanroom blocks, and heavy utilities ready for reuse—exactly what a **module assembly** program needs for a 3–5 GW ramp. Co‑locating the line here does three concrete things for the InterQuest DC thesis:

1. **Removes part of the supply chain risk** your energy partners worry about. If your contracted PV farms are drawing glass/frames domestically and modules from up the road, the **Domestic Content bonus (ITC/PTC +10 points)** is easier for them to claim—and that directly lowers their financing cost and your all‑in delivered energy price.
2. **Activates §45X ($/W) manufacturing credits** at the factory. With transferability, you can program those credits into module pricing for anchor projects feeding your DCs, creating a locally circular advantage that out‑of‑state rivals cannot match quickly.
3. **Shortens the learning loop.** Inline EL/QA and BOM decisions can be tuned against field performance on your own nearby solar fields instead of waiting on distant counterparties.

This is not theoretical; both the site’s power/space and the incentives are public. The constraint is operational discipline—hitting yields and delivery windows—and a **vendor‑agnostic** automation stack that ships product without depending on immature humanoid robotics. ([DataCenterDynamics][8])

## 5) The InterQuest–Eastern Plains–1615 flywheel (how it should actually work)

In practice, the flywheel is a **policy‑aware, physics‑aligned triangle**:

* **Manufacture at 1615**: stand up module lines first to harvest §45X and control module availability and specs relevant to domestic content calculations. Lock early MOUs for domestic glass/frames to simplify your customers’ ITC bonus claims. ([IRS][9])
* **Contract PV on the Plains**: pre‑wire 2–3 utility‑scale projects (200–500 MW each) with developers already active in Colorado (Lightsource bp; NextEra), targeting **mid‑2026–2028 COD** to match your module ramp. Use CSU wheeling or CfD structures to map their output to InterQuest load; do not rely on spot RECs as a substitute. ([Lightsource bp][10])
* **Operate compute to price and carbon**: schedule flexible AI training and batch analytics into **midday “saver” blocks** and weekends; maintain service SLOs with battery‑backed capacity for evening peaks. Design for air‑side economization and set explicit **WUE caps** in the basis‑of‑design. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][11])

The point is not perfection; it’s **credible, near‑term asymmetry**: you get lower effective energy cost during hours when GPUs can be pointed at non‑interactive workloads, you de‑risk module supply for your energy partners, and you sell the resilience narrative that **us‑east‑1** just made expensive again.

## 6) Tradeoffs—stated plainly

| Dimension                        | InterQuest + Eastern CO PV + 1615                                                                          | US‑EAST‑1 style concentration (Northern Virginia analogue)                              | What remains unclear / to de‑risk                                                                                                    |
| -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Outage correlation**           | Different utility, climate, and control‑plane; regionally independent by design.                           | Historically correlated failures across many AWS services; repeated large‑scale events. | Ensure true inter‑region failover of your **own** control planes; don’t recreate us‑east‑1 locally. ([Amazon Web Services, Inc.][1]) |
| **Power availability & pricing** | CSU expanding grid nodes; **time‑of‑day** tariffs with midday saver period aligns with PV.                 | PJM/DOM constraints, peaky prices, siting congestion.                                   | CSU **>10 MW** large‑load rules require long‑dated commitments and collateral—price it in. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][3])         |
| **Renewables proximity**         | Active 200–300 MW‑class PV projects in Pueblo/Lincoln; **Power Pathway** enabling Eastern Plains buildout. | New build increasingly contested; interconnection queues long.                          | Jurisdictional seam (CSU vs PSCo/Xcel) means you must structure wheeling/CfD properly. ([Xcel Energy Colorado Power Pathway][5])     |
| **Network**                      | Multiple carriers and metro fiber; colo incumbents prove reach.                                            | Deepest peering on East Coast but also most congested.                                  | Validate diverse long‑haul paths and back‑to‑back IX connectivity (Denver, Dallas). ([Zayo.com][12])                                 |
| **Climate / cooling**            | Economization hours abundant; dry, cool nights; PUE headroom.                                              | Hot/humid summers reduce economizer run‑time.                                           | Engineer hail/wildfire mitigation; set WUE guardrails. ([Weather.gov][4])                                                            |
| **Supply chain**                 | **1615** provides domestic modules; unlocks **ITC domestic‑content bonus** for projects feeding your DC.   | Imports and long supply lines; policy exposure.                                         | Module yields and QA must be proven early; do not promise what §45X/domestic content can’t deliver. ([IRS][9])                       |

## 7) The uncomfortable bits you need to resolve now (or this dies later)

You do **not** yet have (a) hard commitments from CSU for specific **>10 MW** interconnects and wheeling parameters, (b) signed offtakes with Plains PV developers mapping output to your load profile, (c) a BOD that sets **WUE/PUE/SLO** targets consistent with water constraints, or (d) an internal **multi‑region** control‑plane design that keeps your own services from depending on any single cloud region. The AWS outage was a **control‑plane** failure; if your orchestration, identity, or data fabric anchors in us‑east‑1, you will simply recreate the same vulnerability from Colorado. ([Colorado Public Radio][13])

## 8) A minimal, execution‑ready deal structure

In parallel with the manufacturing ramp at **1615 Garden of the Gods**:

1. **Two‑track power commercial:** negotiate a CSU retail supply with (i) a baseline firm block shaped for steady colocation/serving load and (ii) a flex block indexed to midday saver hours for AI training, with **contract wheeling** or CfD to Eastern Plains PV as it reaches COD. Put minimum‑performance covenants on WUE to protect the social license. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][11])
2. **Anchor PV with developers already winning in Colorado:** lock allocation in **Bighorn‑class** and **Thunder Wolf‑class** follow‑ons, plus **Lincoln County** projects such as **Ebba Solar**, with explicit domestic‑content pathways; swap those modules for 45X‑enabled product from **1615** once your line is validated. ([Lightsource bp][10])
3. **Network diversity baked in:** contract dual long‑haul north–south routes (Front Range) and east–west (I‑70), plus Denver IX presence, with failover tested **outside** of us‑east‑1 control. Zayo and peers can deliver the paths; you must execute the tests. ([Zayo.com][12])

None of this requires a moon‑shot. It requires refusing to carry hidden single‑region dependencies and refusing to pretend that energy tariffs and water are somebody else’s problem.

## 9) What this sells to hyperscalers and colos in one sentence

A **regionally independent** compute cluster in a different utility and climate, with a built‑in **midday price advantage** and a **domestic‑content PV supply** ten miles from the racks—**a concrete hedge** against the next us‑east‑1‑style control‑plane event, with verifiable lower delivered cost during training hours. That is a procurement story that will land.

### References you can cite in external pitches (selected)

* **US‑EAST‑1 outage:** AWS post‑event summary (root cause: automation corrupted DNS for DynamoDB); Reuters on recurrence; The Verge on service impact. ([Amazon Web Services, Inc.][1])
* **InterQuest/Colorado Springs DC presence & fiber:** QTS Colorado Springs; NOVVA; Zayo network. ([QTS Data Centers][14])
* **Power & tariff shape:** CSU Kelker Substation expansion; CSU “Building the Future” infrastructure plan; **Energy Wise** rate with **9 a.m.–1 p.m. saver window**. ([Colorado Springs Utilities][15])
* **Eastern Plains PV & transmission:** Xcel’s **Colorado’s Power Pathway**; **Bighorn Solar (300 MW)**; **Thunder Wolf (≈200 MW + 100 MW storage)**; **Ebba Solar (up to 300 MW)**. ([Xcel Energy Colorado Power Pathway][5])
* **1615 Garden of the Gods site capability:** Former Intel fab listing with **~70–90+ MW** and cleanrooms; DCD coverage. ([LoopNet][16])
* **Policy lever:** **IRS Domestic Content** bonus guidance (+10 percentage points ITC) and **§45X** advanced manufacturing credit overview. ([IRS][9])

## What you still don’t know (and must decide next)

You have not fixed (1) the exact **workload mix** to exploit midday saver pricing; (2) your **cooling topology** and the WUE ceiling you will put in writing; (3) the **interconnection & wheeling** playbook with CSU; (4) the domestic‑content **safe‑harbor** path you intend to hand to developers feeding your load; and (5) the **operational discipline** to keep §45X and domestic‑content claims audit‑proof. These are not academic. Without them, the thesis reads like ambition; with them, it reads like a procurement plan.

---

### Immediate next move that actually changes the risk

Draft and circulate a **three‑party term sheet** (you–CSU–PV developer) that pins down: (a) the baseline firm block and the optional flex block aligned to CSU’s saver/off‑peak hours, (b) the wheeling/CfD mechanism from an Eastern Plains plant with domestic‑content pathway, and (c) the WUE/PUE/SLO targets you will commit to contractually. That single document will flush the unknowns faster than another month of strategy decks.
